Day 23: The API Payload Security Pipeline: Order, Protection Levels and Keys

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 16 • Originally published at hackmd.io

The previous post, The Division of Labor Between XML, JSON and MessagePack, closed by saying the other half of value comes here. Encoded and Encrypted differ by one number on the API payload, and underneath that number are three stages that run in a fixed order, while on the way back the order is more than the same run in reverse.

An ERP system's API has to answer two questions that are independent of each other: what a call's content should be processed into, and who is entitled to decide it may be processed less. The first is a fixed pipeline, the second is one declaration written onto the method. The two are kept apart, and are compared against each other at one stop only, which is access control.

An Encoded value goes out as a Base64 string. Getting from an object to that string takes three stages, each carried by its own interface and each replaceable as a whole:

The latter two names are in the deployment's SystemSettings, while the first is declared by the caller on the API payload of every individual call, with no declaration meaning messagepack. That split is what the previous post's three components and two ways of deciding was about. What can be replaced is each stage's implementation, not the order they run in. The encoding side, ApiPayloadTransformer, is two lines, and the source puts the order at the end of each:

Encryption is not in those two lines. It is one further step the layer outside decides on according to the format: Encoded stops here, and Encrypted goes one stage further.

Compression comes before encryption because compression does nothing at all to ciphertext. The output of good encryption is statistically indistinguishable from random data, and repetition and skew are exactly what a compression algorithm looks for. Running the case study's order FormSchema through both orders shows it:

Swapping the order makes no call fail, and no check anywhere says a word. It just means the compression stage stops doing anything from then on.

The way back is those three steps reversed: decrypt, decompress, deserialize. But what matters about the reverse direction is not that it runs backwards, it is that each stage only ever sees bytes that got past the one before it. The authentication code is verified before decryption, and decryption comes before decompression, so the gzip decompressor never at any point holds a stretch of data that has not been verified.

A decompressor is a parser that accepts arbitrary bytes. Feed it ciphertext with one bit changed and it will start parsing all the same, then throw because it cannot read a structure out of it. This order means it never gets that far.

The three values of PayloadFormat were covered in that same earlier post. What access control looks at is not that one on its own, it is the result of comparing it with another enum, and that one has four values.

Two of the values share a name across the two enums, being Encoded and Encrypted, which makes it easy to read them as two ways of saying the same thing. They are not: one records a fact, the other declares a requirement.

What the four levels ask for, and the kind of method each suits:

How the framework's own methods are distributed across those four is itself a decision: the trust boundary is drawn at HTTPS, and only the handful whose authors judged explicitly that the content must not be visible once inside the server are marked Encrypted. Nothing declares the middle level at present, which is where the format side lands rather than something the level side asks for.

Drawing the boundary at HTTPS does not mean application layer encryption is pointless. HTTPS protects only the stretch where data travels over the network, and it is unwrapped the moment it reaches the server, so every stop that handles it after that sees plain text. A node gets compromised, logging and monitoring keep the content, something is intercepted and replayed across network segments or over a VPN: all of these are real in actual ERP deployments.

Drawing the line at Public buys one thing: a front end with no .NET runtime does not have to implement the whole encryption pipeline before it can reach its first method. The cost is that for calls at that level, value is visible once it is inside the server, and a method needing that layer has to be marked for it.

The test is that the actual format has to be greater than or equal to the declared level: doing more is fine, doing less is refused. What follows is all about remote calls, and a remote call gets past LocalOnly at no format level at all:

Firing at the case study's endpoint, the three refusals each carry their own message:

The other half, that doing more is fine, has been measured too: send an Encoded API payload to the Public list query and it passes, and what comes back is Encoded as well. The server does not touch the format, it wraps the response back up using the value the caller sent, so both directions of one round trip are always the same format.

LocalOnly is not stricter encryption. It asks something else: this method does not arrive over the network at all. Not being on the same axis, it is not compared with the format for size, it is judged on its own. The Connector and the Convergence of Client Responsibilities mentioned that a few server side methods accept in-process calls only, and this is the slot it meant. They can still be called, just not over the network. A local call is let through whole the moment it reaches this check, without the format being looked at, so the table above does not govern them.

This check can sit ahead of decryption because both values it compares are available from the start: the format is a number on the API payload, and the level is attached to the type and fixed once compiled. Neither side requires opening value and looking inside. JSON-RPC 2.0: Protocol Choice and the Request Pipeline said access control sits ahead of restoration and does no decryption work for a request that has not passed, and this is the condition that makes it hold.

A method with no declaration is not unrestricted, it throws. The search order is the method itself, then the one it overrides, then the type declaring it. The cost is that a missing declaration only surfaces when somebody calls it for the first time, so there is a separate build time rule that points such methods out.

The name puts three parts together: a symmetric encryption algorithm, a mode of operation, and a message authentication code. In the implementation it comes to four decisions, and the body of the encrypting and decrypting is AesCbcHmacCryptor:

That fixed time comparison is three lines in the decryption stage:

The message itself hides a choice too. Three different ways of breaking it were measured: one bit changed in the ciphertext, one bit changed in the authentication code, and an entirely unrelated key. All three come back with the same HMAC validation failed.. Being unable to tell them apart is correct. Being able to distinguish a wrong authentication code from a wrong key is itself a signal that can be probed.

Getting all four of the previous section's decisions right still leaves one thing unanswered: who supplies that 64 byte key. The framework keeps this and the previous section in two separate layers:

The dividing line takes one question: does this code know what it is computing? The encryption code in the previous section recognizes only bytes and a key, and does not know it is encrypting an API call, so it lives in the primitive layer. That is also why this post's payload and the key protection coming up use the same code.

The Purpose and Classification of Definition Files reached a conclusion about the startup dependency chain that carries straight over here: the master key is the first item in the chain, and it cannot be encrypted itself, so SystemSettings records only where it is. The framework's default copy, SystemSettings.xml, records the name of an environment variable:

An environment variable is the present default and a file is the other option. Each route has its own exposure surface, and the environment variable is not chosen for being safer. It is chosen because containers and secret management tools inject through environment variables as a matter of course, while going through a file means either mounting a volume or baking the file into the image. The master key does not encrypt any call directly. Its use is unlocking the other keys in the settings, which are stored as ciphertext, and what wraps them is AesCbcHmacCryptor.

Session Management and Lifecycle listed what a session carries, one of them being this connection's symmetric key, and it named this post at the time. That key comes from an interface, and the framework ships three implementations:

What the third column decides is whether rebuilding a session from a database seed, which that same earlier post described, can hold at all. The interface carries a property that answers exactly this: if the key cannot be recovered, a rebuilt session leaves the user looking as though they are still signed in while every encrypted call fails.

The derived one's root key comes from the master key by default, and a value given in the settings takes precedence. The cost of making it the default is that the same access token always derives the same key, rather than a random one at each login. What that buys is a key that never has to be stored and that every node can compute for itself, while the token itself is a GUID nobody can guess and the root key never leaves the server. The order here is counterintuitive as a result: the token has to be generated first, and only then can the key be derived.

At login the caller generates an RSA key pair and writes the public key into the login request. Once the server has built the session it wraps the symmetric key with that public key and returns it alongside the access token. Every call asking for encryption after that uses it.

That same post on the Connector wrote up the exception on the browser side: an RSA key pair cannot be generated there, so the public key sent is an empty string and the server returns an empty string too. The degrading happens on the calling end rather than on the server, and a call asking for Encrypted falls back to Encoded by itself when it has no key in hand. Set that against the second section and it is clear how far this fallback route goes: the Encoded format gets past Public and Encoded, and does not get past the handful marked Encrypted.

The application code in the case study relating to this post is a few lines at startup: put a demo master key in if the environment variable is not set, build the payload options from the definition file into components, then allow automatic creation when the framework starts. The rest is all in the definition files: gzip for compression, aes-cbc-hmac for encryption, and the master key from BEE_MASTER_KEY. For the value slot the case study declares nothing, so it takes messagepack, which is what applies when nothing is declared.

[ApiAccessControl] appears zero times in the whole case study. The order business object overrides GetNewData, and the overriding method carries no declaration, relying on exactly the second section's rule that the search moves on to the method it overrides.

The implementation that does not encrypt is only allowed to be constructed in debug mode, and the case study has debug mode on. The Error Contract and User Visible Messages used the same flag, where what it decided was whether error messages get masked. So that check may as well not exist here, and the case study picked real encryption anyway. The check has a gap of its own as well: it only stops the route that builds an implementation by name, and the route that hands a component in directly goes around it.

What actually makes the case study's encryption not count is not that check, it is the demo master key written into the source. The comment beside it says outright that production has to inject a real one through a real deployment mechanism before startup. The framework cannot tell which kind of environment it is running in, and blocking startup outright would take down test environments wanting to run the sample data, so this is recorded in a comment and nowhere else.

A call's value carries three stages of processing and one declaration:

All three are things anybody can go and check. A pipeline with the order swapped shows it in the compression ratio on the spot. Whether a level holds is answered by firing once at the endpoint for each. Whether the authentication code is stamped in the right place is answered by changing one bit and trying.

One thing is not. Whether that master key really is known only to whoever deployed it cannot be seen from inside the framework, and cannot be measured either. Once the three above are all done right, the strength of the whole thing equals whatever is in that environment variable, and that happens outside the code: the real line of defense is the deployment checklist.

In a security pipeline, the part about whether it is built right can be verified, and the part about whether it deserves trust cannot. The first is a design question, closed once it is written into the mechanism. The second has to be answered again at every deployment.

The next post, The Three Axes of Permissions: Action, Row and Column, changes axis. A protection level governs the form of one call, and at no point does it ask which data this person may see.

Part 3: Building the App & Caching for Data Latency

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Navigation: ← Part 2: Optimizing Dockerfiles with Multi-Stage Builds | Part 4: Full-Cycle CI/CD — From Local Hooks to AWS ECR →

📦 Companion Repository: Complete source code, Dockerfiles, and Compose configurations are available on GitHub: spmahapatra/rick-morty-api-helm.

Platform engineers: Standardise your cache connection patterns. Always use a middleware to automatically generate deterministic cache keys for GET requests.

SREs on-call: Stop silent cascading failures. If your upstream provider goes down, a stale cache fallback prevents your app from crashing.

First-timers: Understand the app flow first. Don't fetch the same data twice. If a user asks for character data, fetch it once, store it in Redis, and serve subsequent requests instantly.

Our application is a Python Flask REST API. Its primary job is to aggregate data from the external Rick & Morty API (https://rickandmortyapi.com/api). Specifically, our business logic filters the data to return characters matching strict criteria: species=human, status=alive, and origin=earth.

Here is the core structure you will find in app.py:

We keep it simple, but we rely heavily on an external upstream API. This introduces a major operational risk: Data Latency.

When a user requests data, the naive flow is: User -> Our Flask App -> Upstream Rick & Morty API -> Our Flask App -> User.

To solve this, we introduced the CacheMiddleware backed by Redis.

Here is the architectural flow of a request:

If you look at src/cache/detection.py in our repository, you will see how we intercept requests before they hit our business logic.

We must uniquely identify every request to store it correctly. We generate a cache key based on the HTTP method, endpoint, and query parameters:

The CacheMiddleware hooks into Flask's before_request and after_request lifecycle.

If Redis crashes, our application must not crash. We wrap our cache connection in try/except blocks. If Redis is unreachable, we simply log a warning and fall back to fetching data directly from the upstream API.

Symptom: When a highly requested cache key expires (like a popular character or homepage data), 100 concurrent requests miss the cache at the exact same millisecond. They all hit the upstream API simultaneously, causing a rate limit block or an upstream crash.

Root Cause: Because the key is gone, all application threads independently decide they need to fetch and hydrate the cache.

Fix (Soft TTL / Background Refresh): Instead of waiting for a hard TTL expiration, implement a "Soft TTL". Store the data alongside an expiration timestamp. When a request comes in, check if the Soft TTL has passed. If it has, immediately return the stale data to the user, but spawn a background thread (using Celery or FastAPI BackgroundTasks) to fetch fresh data and update the cache. This ensures only one thread hits the upstream API, while users never experience the 300ms latency penalty.

If you cache everything endlessly, Redis will run out of memory (OOM). You must configure an eviction policy in your redis.conf. For an API cache, allkeys-lru (Least Recently Used) is usually the best choice. When Redis hits its memory limit, it will automatically delete the cache keys that haven't been accessed recently, making room for new data while keeping the most popular characters in memory.

This application is designed as a drop-in reference architecture. If you need a fully-functional, containerised application to test your local Kubernetes clusters, CI/CD pipelines, or logging stacks, you can use this repository as your baseline.

Here is exactly how to deploy it, query it, and validate the caching mechanism locally.

Clone the companion repository (spmahapatra/rick-morty-api-helm) and spin up the Flask application alongside the Redis cache:

This starts two containers: your api service (exposed on port 5000) and your redis cache backend.

Use curl or Postman to request the character data. The first request will take longer as the app reaches out to the upstream provider:

Look closely at the HTTP response headers. You will see X-Cache: MISS.

Run the exact same curl command again. The response should return almost instantly:

The response headers will now show X-Cache: HIT, proving that the data was served directly from your local Redis container, completely bypassing the public internet.

If you want to validate the raw JSON payloads stored in memory, you can connect directly to the Redis container using redis-cli:

Pitfall 1: Unhandled Redis connection timeouts

If we scale our Flask app to 5 containers, a local dictionary means we have 5 separate, unsynchronised caches. Redis provides a centralised, shared cache for all containers, ensuring consistent data and memory efficiency.

We enforce a strict Time-To-Live (TTL) on our Redis keys. Once the TTL expires, the key is evicted, forcing the next request to fetch fresh data from the upstream API. This balances low latency with eventual consistency.

You can build a backdoor into your middleware. Look for a specific header like Cache-Control: no-cache in the incoming request, and if present, force the app to skip the Redis check and go straight to the upstream API.

Kubernetes manifests and Helm chart packaging are scheduled for Part 5 of this series. For now, run and test the application locally using Docker Compose (docker compose up -d).

How to Flip a Video With FFmpeg (hflip and vflip Are Mirrors, Not Rotates, and -c copy Cannot Do Them)

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Short answer: to flip a video with FFmpeg, re-encode through hflip or vflip:

hflip is a left–right mirror. vflip is a top–bottom mirror. The frame size stays the same (here, 720×1280). That is not a rotate: transpose=1 would turn this clip into 1280×720. And despite what autocomplete offers for "ffmpeg flip video without re encoding", a flip is a video filter, so -c:v copy is rejected: Filtering and streamcopy cannot be used together. Audio can still be stream-copied. Below are pixel-exact measurements on a real 8-second clip, a small Python module, and five pytest checks.

The fixture is a real generated example from Voor's Enzo Fernandez Celebration page: a vertical 720×1280 H.264 + AAC clip, 24 fps, 193 frames, 8.041667 s, with stadium crowd sound. MD5 of the file is 3f8a3a6abc3da386c4d6628e6ff56ff9.

(Tested with FFmpeg 9.0.1, Python 3.13, pytest 9.2.0.)

ffmpeg flip video 90 degrees shows up in US-English autocomplete next to the horizontal/vertical queries. A 90° rotate changes the axes. A flip does not.

The first command still writes a 720×1280 frame. transpose=1 would write 1280×720. On the raw RGB24 first frame, hflip is exactly the Python left–right mirror of the source — every row reversed, zero differing bytes. vflip is exactly the top–bottom mirror. If you wanted the clip upside down and backwards left-to-right, that is hflip,vflip, and it is byte-identical to rotate=PI (a 180° rotate). It is not a 90° rotate.

"ffmpeg flip video without re encoding" is a real autocomplete suggestion. This is what FFmpeg 9.0.1 does with it:

The process exits non-zero (234 here) and writes no file. A horizontal flip has to rewrite every macroblock; the original bitstream cannot be copied. There is no lossless -c:v copy path for hflip or vflip. If you only needed to tag a player to mirror at display time, that would be a different (and poorly supported) metadata trick — and it would not change the pixels, which is what a flip is supposed to do.

The video has to be re-encoded. The audio does not. With -c:a copy the AAC bitstream is identical to the source: MD5 5843435af71daac1e832f8b54fe25bdf on both the original and the flipped file, 131354 bytes. You keep the stadium crowd exactly as it was, and you do not pay a second AAC encode.

Flipping twice is also exact on the raw frame: hflip,hflip returns the original 720×1280 RGB24 first frame with zero differing bytes. That is a useful sanity check before you trust a longer filter chain.

flip.py wraps the operations the tests actually use: decode the first frame to raw RGB24 (optionally through a filter), build a Python mirror to compare against, attempt a stream-copy flip (which must fail), and re-encode a flipped file while copying the audio.

test_flip.py uses the same fixture. The first four tests decode raw frames, so they are not affected by a later H.264 encode:

The clip is a real Enzo Fernandez Celebration example generated on Voor: one photo becomes an 8-second 9:16 run to the corner flag, hands behind the ears, stadium crowd included. With the official published input still in the form, the generator quoted 469 credits for a public, watermarked clip on November 16, 2026. If you want your own clip to practice on, the Enzo Fernandez Celebration generator is where this one came from — then flip it with the commands above. (The page is an independent trend tool, not affiliated with Enzo Fernández or any club or broadcaster. Use a photo of yourself, a pet, or someone who agreed.)

Firecracker for Agent Code Execution Requires Building and Operating the Sandbox Service

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 16 • Originally published at effloow.com

We care about sandbox costs because agent execution turns small unit prices into a material infrastructure bill. A coding agent does not run one command. It installs dependencies, retries failed tests, holds a workspace between turns, and sometimes waits while another model reasons. At volume, the lifetime of those environments matters as much as their CPU consumption.

Firecracker provides a KVM-backed isolation primitive, but a self-hosted sandbox still requires an execution service, security controls, and infrastructure operations.

Our question was simple: can we replace a managed sandbox bill with infrastructure we control without accidentally becoming a sandbox-platform company?

Firecracker supplies an important part of that answer. On the KVM path we evaluate here, it runs a guest Linux kernel inside a lightweight virtual machine. Its deliberately small device model reduces exposed functionality compared with a general-purpose VMM. We get a hardware virtualization boundary rather than asking untrusted programs to share the host kernel directly.

We do not get a complete agent execution service.

We still need image builds, a guest execution agent, admission control, workspace handling, deadlines, output limits, network policy, cleanup, and an authenticated customer-facing API. Firecracker's host API configures machines; it does not provide an equivalent of sandbox.commands.run().

Our evidence boundary matters: we reviewed the implementation-facing material and external execution accounts, but did not complete a local KVM deployment for this article. Our review environment lacked the necessary KVM access, binaries, images, and privileged networking controls. The walkthrough below is a reproduction recipe, with explicitly simulated output—not a claim that we measured boot latency or validated production isolation.

That distinction changes our recommendation. We consider Firecracker a credible primitive for a self-hosted sandbox, not a deployment we can certify from a successful API response. Our starting references are the Firecracker project, its quickstart, and its production host guidance.

We separate the first experiment from the production design. The first experiment should prove that a trusted guest boots on the intended host. It should not accept customer code.

We require Linux with working /dev/kvm, a supported CPU architecture, and permission for the VMM identity to access KVM. A cloud instance being “a Linux VM” is insufficient: its provider and instance configuration must expose hardware virtualization.

Our initial inventory is deliberately mundane:

We would run the permission checks as the intended service identity, not only as root.

We install Firecracker and jailer from the same pinned release, using the upstream release archive for the host architecture. We verify the release artifacts before installation and retain their hashes. We do not use an unversioned download or assume that a URL ending in a binary name exists for every release.

Our build path would use a pinned Linux source tree and Firecracker's matching guest configuration as the kernel baseline. We would build the appropriate kernel target for the architecture, rather than assume one image format fits every host.

For the filesystem, we would construct a minimal distribution tree, install the interpreter and guest agent, add an init service, and package it into ext4. For example, once a trusted staging tree exists:

The 1 GiB file size is an example build choice, not a measured storage requirement. These commands package a tree; they do not magically create its init system or execution agent.

We would first boot upstream quickstart artifacts, then replace the kernel and filesystem separately. That keeps a broken image build from masquerading as a KVM problem.

Here is our proposed trusted-guest smoke test, which we have not executed or validated against a pinned Firecracker API specification. Before running it, we would check the endpoints, payloads, and lifecycle sequence against the selected release and supply verified, compatible guest artifacts. We intentionally leave networking out.

This launches Firecracker directly, without jailer. We would never use this diagnostic path to run untrusted agent code.

A successful start response does not prove that init completed. A read-only guest also needs appropriate writable temporary filesystems for its services. We would inspect the serial log and then require an actual command response from the guest agent.

Our production path would start with the jailer reference, matched to the pinned binary.

We need a unique instance ID, an explicit unprivileged UID/GID, a controlled chroot base, configured resource controls, and any required network namespace. Kernel, disk, and socket paths must make sense inside the jail. Host absolute paths from the diagnostic script cannot simply be copied into jailed API requests.

We would verify the resulting UID, namespace membership, cgroup placement, device access, and file ownership before admitting a job. Starting through jailer is a step in the security model, not proof that the whole model is correct.

Our environment lacked usable KVM and privileged host controls, so we could not validate boot, jailer behavior, or enforcement. We stopped there rather than replace those checks with a mock socket and call the result a sandbox test.

The remaining issues are concrete deployment risks we identified, not failures we personally reproduced.

We still have to build the guest agent. It must validate request lengths, manage subprocesses, separate stdout and stderr, enforce output caps, return exit status, and survive malformed requests.

We would run execution workers without guest-root privileges. We would also retain a host-side deadline that destroys the entire microVM. A compromised guest must not be able to negotiate away its termination.

Vsock can keep command transport separate from IP networking, but it does not make guest responses trustworthy. We would treat every returned byte as untrusted input.

We would audit this distinction first when testing resource controls.

A host cgroup's pids.max bounds host tasks, including VMM threads. It does not count every process running inside the guest. We need guest-side process controls as well.

We would not copy a “128 MB sandbox” setting into a 128 MB host memory ceiling and assume it works. Under load, we would inspect cgroup memory events, CPU throttling, host pressure, and guest failures together.

A network namespace is not an egress policy. NAT is not an authorization boundary.

Our first untrusted execution profile would have no virtual network interface. If package installation becomes necessary, we would add a separately tested network path with explicit policy for:

We would avoid the common quickstart-to-production shortcut of broad forwarding and masquerading rules. Those rules establish connectivity; they do not establish safe connectivity.

We have no local snapshot-restore measurement or verified restore sequence to publish. We would use the pinned release's snapshot documentation rather than improvise endpoints.

Our acceptance tests would cover artifact compatibility, backing disks, namespace and TAP reconstruction, stale connections, guest clocks, and readiness after restore. Snapshots containing credentials or customer state would be sensitive artifacts.

We would also test entropy and identity regeneration when cloning prepared state. We would not fork a memory image containing reusable secrets and assume each child becomes independent automatically.

A small VMM does not guarantee a fast application environment. General-purpose init services, device waits, SSH startup, and runtime imports can dominate the path to useful execution.

Our workaround would be a minimal, observable boot chain—not blanket removal of randomness-related services. We would measure where startup blocks before changing entropy handling or weakening guest configuration.

We cannot publish a raw Firecracker p50 or p95 from this review. We have no completed local run.

In our managed sandbox comparison, we use 717 ms create and 662 ms resume as E2B's command-executed readiness baseline. These timings describe the managed service, not raw Firecracker boot latency; our local KVM reproduction remained unperformed.

We would reject a comparison that puts a local InstanceStart acknowledgment beside that command-ready measurement.

We would benchmark four paths separately: fresh boot, snapshot restore, warm-pool allocation, and a subsequent command in an existing guest.

We would start the timer when we admit a request and stop it when we receive a nonce-bearing command result. We would report p50, p95, failures, and timeouts under increasing concurrency. Artifact preparation and queue delay would remain visible rather than disappear outside the timer.

A warm pool can reduce allocation delay, but it consumes capacity before requests arrive. It is not evidence of a faster cold boot.

Consider illustrative assumptions, not current vendor quotes:

That is roughly 36.5 continuously occupied sandboxes over a 30-day month. It is not a capacity claim for those three hosts. We would still have to prove that the chosen CPU, RAM, I/O, and failure reserve can support the workload.

Ignoring labor lowers the apparent threshold to 11,250 hours. That is precisely how a self-hosting proposal becomes misleading.

We would also amortize initial engineering separately. Burstiness, idle sessions, retention, billing semantics, and negotiated pricing can change the answer substantially.

Our verdict: shortlist Firecracker as an isolation primitive; do not mistake it for a finished agent sandbox.

Our next validation step would use one hardened host, one pinned artifact set, and a destructive acceptance suite: guest memory exhaustion, fork pressure, output flooding, disk filling, timeout enforcement, controller crashes, denied network probes, and snapshot compatibility failures.

Until those checks pass, “self-hosted isolation” is an architecture proposal—not a production guarantee.

We keep related infrastructure evaluations in our tools collection. If the real decision is whether to build this control plane or buy an execution service, our architecture services focus on that boundary: the workload, the threat model, and the operational bill we will actually have to pay.

My most-read page has 1,322,170 reads. My site has had 4 outbound clicks.

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

My daily writing queue is a list of the "most-read" pages on my AI tools directory. Today it put Deevid AI at #1 with 1,322,170 reads, Text to Video AI: Deevid at #2 with 983,454, and Deevid AI Image at #3 with 637,094.

Those are three landing pages for one product, and my site has produced 4 outbound clicks in its entire history. Here is how I found out, and what the number actually is.

The directory has ~4,300 tool listings, imported by a crawler that reads another AI tool directory's public listing pages. In scripts/crawler.mjs the field is extracted like this:

That selector is a popularity counter on someone else's page. It is stored in a column whose own schema comment says "Popularity counter mirrored from the source directory". Nothing in the pipeline ever mixed it with a request log from my domain.

So the "reads" number in my queue is not my traffic. It is an inherited signal about how much attention a product gets on the site I scraped — and I treated it as if it were mine.

This is the part I underestimated. The number is not just an internal ranking input; it is rendered to visitors as if it described my page:

A visitor has no way to know the difference between "this product is popular on the site we imported data from" and "this page was read 1.3 million times on ours". The second one is a claim about my site. I have never made it deliberately, and I could not support it if asked — the field that would support a claim about my site, an outbound click counter, sums to 4 across all 4,323 rows.

Three rows, three URLs, same vendor: deevid.ai/image-to-video, deevid.ai/text-to-video, deevid.ai/ai-image-generator. Between them they hold 2,942,718 of the counter's units, which is 13.4% of everything the top 150 rows carry. They also carry inconsistent pricing labels — two rows say free, one says freemium — because each was scraped independently and nothing reconciled them.

If I spend my hour writing body copy in queue order, my first three days of work are three variations of the same page for the same company, ranked #1 partly because that vendor's parent directory counts each landing page separately.

The obvious fix is to group listings by domain and only let the highest-scoring one occupy a slot. I tried it in SQL against the current data, and it fails in both directions:

The identifier I'd need — "which product is this" — is exactly the thing the import never captured. It's the same reason my rebrand cleanup is manual: the rows have unique URLs, so URL uniqueness proves nothing about product uniqueness.

Summed over the 4,176 listings that have any value, the counter totals 49,736,374. The top 150 rows — 3.5% of the directory — account for 22,025,504, i.e. 44.3%. Inside that top band the differences are noise: row 150 sits at 44,554 and row 151 at 44,250. A gap of 304 counter-units, out of 44,554, decides whether a page gets written proper copy or stays a template sentence.

Worse, 147 listings have the counter at exactly 0. They are last in every ordering forever, no matter what they are. And because this field is copied from a third-party page, I have no way to tell whether a value is current, stale, or a scraping artifact — when I tried to re-check a handful of source pages today I got an HTTP 403, so I could not even confirm a single number externally.

Being precise about what is done versus planned, because "here's my fix" sections usually lie:

The lesson generalizes past my directory: if a metric is inherited, label it as inherited at the point of display, not just in the schema comment. Nobody reads the comment. Everybody reads the number.

If you want to see what the ranking currently produces, the category leaderboards are at aiverse.mindutil.com/best — the ordering there is this same counter, which is now the honest way to describe it.

The pros and cons of using cloud storage over local storage

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Cloud storage can be super convenient, but there are times when using local storage is the better move.

External hard drives and USB flash drives are usually easy ways to back up your files, especially since they don't require an internet connection or waiting for uploads. You just plug them into your computer, copy the files you need, and that's it. But cloud services like iCloud and Google Drive have also made it a lot easier to back up everything — and most of the time, it all happens automatically.

Your photos, videos, contacts and more are all synced to the cloud without having to copy each file manually. Plus, there's no risk of leaving your external drive somewhere or damaging it to the point where you lose your files. That doesn't mean cloud storage is perfect, and both have their pros and cons — it really comes down to choosing what works best for your needs. If you're unsure whether to choose local or cloud storage to back up your data, here's how the two stack up on access, cost and reliability.

One of the biggest advantages of using cloud storage is the convenience of not having to worry about your files. Once you upload them to the cloud, they're with you wherever you go. Just log in to your account on another laptop or phone, and you can access everything in just a few minutes. It's perfect for people who use multiple devices or who need to access files from their home computer at the office or college. Plus, most cloud services let you have your files synced automatically. That's the case with iCloud — once you enable it on your device, it automatically backs up everything every day.

Sharing files through a cloud service is also much easier than sharing them from local storage. Once a file is uploaded to the cloud, you can easily share it with someone with just a click  — cloud platforms generate a link so others can view and download that file. There's also collaboration, so multiple people can work on the same document or spreadsheet at the same time. Platforms like Google Docs even offer tools that allow each collaborator to leave comments in real time, perfect for workplaces and schools.

Safety is another big plus, since major cloud providers keep copies of your data spread across multiple servers and data centers. The chances of something bad happening and you losing all your data are very low – though not impossible. Plus, your cloud account can be protected with a password and two-factor authentication, meaning others can't just take your files from you.

Cloud storage relies on a stable internet connection, and you're prone to unexpected outages. A slow connection or an outage on your provider's servers could prevent you from downloading a file when you need it most. Also, uploading and downloading large files can take a while if you're using a slow connection. With an external SSD, you can copy gigabytes of files in just minutes. Internet has no impact on local storage, so it's something to consider if you're always traveling and rely on mobile data, for example.

Cost is another reason that might push people away from cloud services. While you only have to pay once to buy an external hard drive, cloud providers typically charge recurring subscription fees on a monthly or annual basis. Apple charges $9.99 per month in the US for 2TB of iCloud+, which comes to nearly $240 if you pay for two years — and the storage isn't really yours. Stop paying, and your files may be deleted after a while, not to mention possible price increases down the road. By comparison, you can find 2TB SSDs for around $290, but they'll always be yours, with no extra charges.

That doesn't mean you should ditch the cloud and rely entirely on local storage. Subscribing to a cloud service that allows you to back up all your files and access them from anywhere is very handy, and you can also benefit from local storage by offloading some of your larger files or those you really want to make sure you never lose.

While it’s been over three decades since the final Full House episode aired, DJ Tanner actress Candace Cameron Bure still feels her TV “family” is important to her. So, you can only imagine how emotional the TV actress’s tribute was for her TV dad, Bob Saget, on the fourth anniversary of his death this year. Just your average South Floridian cinephile who believes the pen is mightier than the sword.

10 things your iPhone's Notes app can do other than taking notes

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Apple Notes has a lot of tricks you might not have properly explored.

For most people, the Notes app is where grocery lists, stray thoughts and various codes go to live. If you've only used it for basic text, you've probably missed how Apple has added new features to the mix that can solve complex equations, draw graphs, transcribe audio and turn your doodles into images. iOS 27 brought even more, including smarter Siri integration and divider lines, so the app is more capable than ever.

A few of the features below rely on Apple Intelligence, requiring newer hardware to work as intended. On the iPhone, that means the iPhone 15 Pro, iPhone 15 Pro Max and any iPhone 16 model or later. Those features are flagged along the way, along with anything that requires a specific version of iOS.

The Notes app has a calculator hiding inside it, with the math happening while you're typing. Since iOS 18, when you type an equation and add an equals sign, Math Notes fills in the answer on the spot. It also works with variables, so if you write "dinner = $42" and change that value later, every result that uses it updates automatically. That makes it a surprisingly easy way to split the bill or plan a monthly budget.

Math Notes can do more than give you simple answers. After you type an equation that starts with "y =", tap the equals sign and choose Insert Graph to plot what you've typed right inside the note. If you change the variable, the graph changes automatically to match. It's a neat tool for students to check their homework without a dedicated graphing app.

If you don't have a physical scanner at home, there's no need to download a dedicated scanner app to replace it. Tapping the attachment button in any note shows the Scan Documents option, where Notes will capture a page automatically, including cropping and straightening it for you. The scanner also offers a flash option and image filters if needed.

In Auto mode, you don't even need to tap on the shutter button; the system captures the document once it detects the page's edges and you're holding your iPhone steady. You can scan multiple pages into a single document, which is useful for contracts, lease agreements or anything else that runs longer than a page.

If the document needs a signature, open the scan, tap Markup, then go to Add > Signature. Use a saved signature or draw a new one with your finger, then resize it and drag it into place. It's an easy way to sign and send a form without taking a printout of the pages. Scans are saved to the note as PDFs, which you can annotate.

Notes can double as a recorder: choose Record Audio in any note to record and transcribe everything your phone hears. The transcript is searchable, so you can jump into the relevant part of a long lecture instead of scrubbing through the entire session. On iPhone models with Apple Intelligence, Notes can summarize entire recorded lectures or meetings. Phone calls you record also land in Notes inside the Call Recordings folder with a transcript.

The iPhone already has a dedicated recording app in Voice Memos, so it's worth knowing when to use which. Notes is the better pick when the recording is part of something bigger. For a class or a meeting, for example, you can type your own notes, add photos of the whiteboard and keep the recording all in the same note. Everything stays together, so you won't have to switch between multiple apps when you're going through your notes at home. Meanwhile, Voice Memos makes more sense when the audio itself matters most, like an interview you plan to edit or share.

If you need some help when drawing, sketch something rough with the Markup tools, then pick Image Wand from the same tool palette and circle your sketch. Add a few words describing the image you want, and Apple Intelligence will turn your doodle into a polished image.

You also have the option to circle an empty spot in the note and create a new image based on the text around it. Afterward, fine-tune the image by adding or removing descriptive words. If your note is a packing list for a beach trip, for instance, it will generate a matching seaside illustration to sit at the top.

In iOS 27, Image Playground, which powers this feature in the Notes app, got a new model that can make photorealistic images, not just stylized ones. Since iOS 27.2, most images you generate or edit carry a SynthID watermark, which the SynthID detection website uses to know something was made by AI.

Your notes don't have to be only for you; it's possible to share a specific note or an entire folder with your family, friends and coworkers, then everyone can make changes in real time. This is ideal for a household grocery list or a group plan for a trip. The person who creates the shared note remains in control of who can do what.

Just tap the Share button in the note you want to add people to, then choose whether each person can make changes or only view it. Type @ followed by someone's name and they'll get a notification pointing them to the note. If several people are editing the same note, the activity view and highlights show who changed what and when. And in iOS 27, the Shared folder now sits at the top of the folders list with a new icon, so multi-person notes are easier to spot.

The Notes app can help with the actual writing you do. Select some text, tap the Apple Intelligence button in the toolbar and pick an option from the Writing Tools menu. This can proofread your text for spelling and grammar mistakes and rewrite it in a friendlier, more professional or more concise tone.

The suite is also useful for reshaping messy notes. It's able to create a summary of a long note or pull out its key points, which is handy after a meeting or before an exam. It will also turn a block of text into a bulleted list or even convert it into a table. If you've jotted down a few phone plans with their prices and data limits, for example, the Table option can organize them into neat rows and columns so they're easier to compare.

Writing Tools isn't limited to just the Notes app; it works almost anywhere you can type on the iPhone, including third-party apps. In iOS 27, Apple Intelligence also suggests ways to improve your writing.

Long notes quickly become messy and hard to organize, but Notes has a few tools to keep them neat. With iOS 18 and newer, you have the option to collapse any heading to minimize sections you're not working on. This means the final version is a bit cleaner to view, while it's also easier to manage everything while writing.

If that's not enough, you can also highlight text in different colors, curating them by sections, information or any other preference. To do so, select the text, tap the Aa format button and pick a color. Notes offers five color options to choose from: pink, purple, orange, mint and blue. iOS 27 also added divider lines, which gives another option to tidy up a mess. Place your cursor where you want a break, open the edit menu and choose Insert Divider Line. If someone views the note on iOS 26 or prior, the divider lines show up as three hyphens(—), so nothing breaks.

iOS 27 also added section links to the mix, letting you link to a specific section of a note where tapping the link jumps straight there. That's particularly useful for a table of contents in a long note. Pasting Markdown now converts it to formatted text automatically, and a new Copy as Markdown option easily brings notes to other apps with the formatting intact.

iOS 26 added a reed pen to the Markup tools, which is designed for a traditional calligraphy experience. Like the actual writing instrument, your strokes get thicker or thinner depending on the direction you draw in. It's a unique way to write, especially for special occasions like birthdays and invitations. You'll find the reed pen in the list of Markup tools; tapping it again after selecting it is how you pick a preset.

There are five angle presets to choose from, with the one you pick deciding which stroke direction gives the thickest line. The angle is fixed no matter how you hold your finger, so your strokes stay consistent. Apart from the Notes app, the reed pen also works in Freeform, Journal and Preview — basically anywhere the Markup tools are available.

The search bar in the Notes app is smarter than it looks and than how most people casually use it. Beyond your own typed notes, it can search handwriting, objects in images and text in scanned documents. Tap the search button, then type a word from a page or a photo. If you scanned a receipt months ago, searching for the store's name should bring it up. It's an easy way to find snippets from longer notes, especially when you're transcribing lectures or meetings.

This turns your Notes library into a practical place to store paperwork you might need in the future. Consider storing information like warranty cards, appliance manuals, prescriptions and receipts all in Notes, then you can find them by searching for a word on the page instead of scrolling through dozens of scans and navigating file names.

Siri and Notes finally work together properly in iOS 27. Siri can create notes with generated content, like information it pulled from the web, instead of only typing what you say. Ask it to put together a packing list for a trip and save it to a new note, and it will comply. This really comes in handy for smaller notes or basic reminders, as well as capturing ideas on the spot when you can't stop to type.

It can also add to an existing note or clean up a list before saving it. If you've got a messy, unformatted list, ask Siri to tidy it up and add it to the right note. Of course, the full power of this requires Siri AI, which launched in English first. iOS 27.2 added French, Japanese, Korean, Portuguese and Spanish. Older iPhone models can also create notes with Siri, but without the AI-generated content, which slightly limits the feature. But the basics are still there to jot down ideas.

I Just Learned There’s A Project Hail Mary Video Game, And Rocky’s Role Sounds ‘Amaze’

I Rewatched The End Of Oak Street, And Yes, That Is A Battlestar Galactica Reference

Sylvester Stallone Was Ready To Be ‘Hypercritical’ Of I Play Rocky… Then He Saw The Movie

Should You Read Sunrise On The Reaping Ahead Of The New Hunger Games Movie? The Director And Star Weigh In

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Fans are really going to want to try this out.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Plenty of films left impressions on the 2026 movie schedule, but few managed to really hit the zeitgeist like Project Hail Mary did. Phil Lord and Chris Miller’s science-fiction epic – based on Andy Weir’s 2021 book of the same name – managed to capture audiences’ imaginations (and make some solid money at the box office in the process). Anyone who needs further proof of the film’s popularity should know that a mixed-reality game is in the works and, to quote Rocky, the description of his role sounds “amaze.”

This new game – Project Hail Mary: Journey Among the Stars – hails from game studio Maze Theory and takes place during a formerly untapped period of time within the main story. It specifically takes place after Dr. Ryland Grace saves his alien companion, Rocky, and before they return to the latter’s homeworld, Enid. Players take on the role of Dr. Ryland Grace (played by the impeccable and expressive Ryan Gosling in the film) and, as SFX Magazine on CinemaBlend learned from the developers, the game gets quite immersive.

Players can interact with objects, steer the titular ship (Hail Mary), repair it and even complete tasks like opening cupboards. One also only needs a headset to further immerse themselves in the game’s story, which was also talked out with Weir. Of course, all the while, fans can also interact with Rocky (who is not a pet) throughout their travels, and he’ll be actively watching your moves and providing feedback. Gameplay lead Sandy Lockie told SFX this about bringing Rocky and Grace’s relationship to the game:

We wanted to do justice to Rocky and Grace and their relationship. Sometimes they’ll be crabby with each other, sometimes they’ll get a little bit down and need the other one to pick them up.

It just wouldn’t have made sense to craft a game like this without including Rocky in some form or fashion. Since the film’s release he’s become something of a sensation, and he went viral after Amazon posted a video of the character watching himself. Even Neil DeGrasse Tyson praised the character’s design and noted that Weir and the filmmakers avoid the Hollywood trope of making him a humanoid. Judging by Lockie’s comments, even the Maze Theory team was taken with Rocky while they were testing the game:

Even when we’re testing, although you know he’s a character and isn’t really there, I’ll talk to him: ‘Well, what do I do here?’ He has good suggestions and sometimes bad suggestions. It’s one of those things that your brain just kind of lunges into, like, ‘Yep, Rocky’s there. He’s a character now!’ He’ll do jazz hands or prompt you to play rock-paper-scissors and all sorts of stuff . It’s fun to step into that and feel like he’s your friend and companion, your little explorer buddy.

All in all, this game should provide fans with a bit of a taste of being in Ryland’s shoes and present the opportunity to catch up with Rocky in some sense. Project Hail Mary’s ending may have closed the book on Grace and Rocky’s bromance for now, but I still like the idea of people being able to create their own moments reminiscent of the film’s best scenes. So, on that note, why not jump into this sweet-sounding extension of the PHM story?

Project Hail Mary: Journey Among the Stars is now available Pico, Meta and Android XR. Also, fans can grab a Prime Video subscription and stream the movie on which the game is based right now.

Marvell’s Trillion-Dollar Target

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Custom silicon company Marvell wants to acquire Lumentum at $115 billion, dwarfing many previous deals in the publicly traded tech space.

In this episode of Motley Fool Hidden Gems Investing, Motley Fool contributors Jon Quast, Matt Frankel, and Rachel Warren discuss:

Matt Frankel: I've been following Lumentum since before I was really a big data center player. Lumentum, they're an optical company. They make lasers, they make optical components. While it's significant for data centers, traditionally, electronic signals have been sent through copper wire. That's how, things go around. For without getting too technical, we are reaching the technological limits of that, and Lumentum produces components optical components that turn electric signals into light. It makes it go around data centers faster, more efficiently, higher capacity, things of that nature.

Really, they're the optical component provider. They're the leader in that field. Videos a big investor in them. They can't make these things fast enough, which we'll get to in a little bit. But there's a lot to like about this business. It's growing super fast. Revenue grew 137% year over year in the latest quarter. It's accelerating. It's expecting to grow 27% sequentially. When they report their fourth-quarter earnings, very high margins. Like I said, they have incredible pricing power right now. We can dive into the business in a little while, but that's Lumentum in a nutshell, and Barbell seems very interested in them.

Jon Quast: Across Silicon Valley, tech CEOs can be heard quoting Maverick. I feel the need for speed. All right, I made that part up. But AI does need those fast connections and Lumentum is one of the enablers. But Rachel, I want to talk to you here. I want to turn it to you about Marvell, not Marvel, Marvell. Their key part of their business, their secret weapon, if you will, is their digital signal processors or DSPs. These are specialized silicon that's what's needed to translate data into the clean electrical pulses before it ever hits a laser. Marvell excels at designing these custom AI accelerators that are tailored for the likes of Alphabet and AWS who want proprietary hardware.

This is a rare material. It's critical for making the hyper-fast lasers that are used in AI. Lumentum assembles the optical transceivers, those hardware boxes you see lining the walls of the massive AI server racks. Historically relied quite heavily on traditional telecom companies as their core client base. Now, of course, we've seen a massive shift in their growth story over the last few years, very much reliant on Cloud hyperscalers. They've really shifted their entire business model. I think Marvell sees tremendous potential. They're looking to build their own high-speed optical tech to challenge the likes of Broadcom, and I think that they see Lumentum as a path to achieving that new and revitalized growth story and maybe becoming an alternative for the Cloud giants that maybe don't want to be locked into Broadcom's ecosystem.

Etsy vs. Five Below: Which Growth Stock Is the Better Buy?

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Etsy and Five Below take very different routes to growth, and the gap in their recent numbers may tip the scales for long-term investors.

Choosing between e-commerce innovation and traditional retail growth involves weighing different business models. This comparison examines whether Etsy (ETSY +0.80%) or Five Below (FIVE -0.19%) offers the more compelling opportunity for investors today.

Etsy connects millions of independent sellers with buyers seeking creative items, while Five Below operates high-energy physical stores selling trend-right products at extreme value. Both companies target discretionary spending but use vastly different methods to reach their core audiences.

Etsy operates a global online marketplace that specializes in unique and creative goods, connecting millions of active sellers with approximately millions of active buyers. The company focuses on a two-sided marketplace model where it provides the infrastructure for artisans and vintage collectors to reach a massive audience. To support its vast technical needs, the company relies on third-party service providers like Google Cloud and Amazon (AMZN -0.29%), and it is currently finalized the sale of its Depop business to eBay (EBAY +0.65%).

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $2.9 billion, which represents a modest growth rate compared to the prior year. Net income for this period was approximately $987.5 million. This financial performance highlights the company's ability to maintain its scale within the broader landscape of retail stocks even as consumer habits shift.

Five Below is a specialty value retailer that primarily targets kids, pre-teens, and teens, offering a wide variety of products mostly priced between $1 and $5. As of early 2026, the company operated 1,921 stores across 46 states, creating an immersive shopping experience across eight merchandise "worlds." The business model relies on a network of approximately 1,000 vendors to source trend-right products that appeal to its young demographic and their parents.

In the 12 months ended Aug. 1, 2026, revenue reached $5.3 billion, representing growth of 25.6% over the preceding twelve-month period. Net income for this same timeframe was approximately $619.2 million. This significant growth is largely driven by the company's aggressive store expansion strategy and its ability to maintain popularity among cost-conscious consumers.

Etsy faces intense competition within the retail and e-commerce sectors, particularly from larger platforms that can offer lower prices or faster shipping. The company is also highly dependent on search engines and social media platforms to drive traffic to its marketplace. Furthermore, Etsy must manage complex regulatory and legal risks related to data privacy, artificial intelligence, and intellectual property while navigating potential global supply chain disruptions.

Five Below must navigate risks associated with inflation and rising costs for commodities and labor, which can pressure its low-price business model. The company's reliance on a global supply chain makes it vulnerable to tariff impacts and logistics delays. Additionally, Five Below faces challenges in managing its rapid expansion, including the risks of inventory management, retail shrink, and federal class action lawsuits related to its growth projections.

Etsy appears more attractively priced based on future earnings estimates, though both companies show differences when comparing the price-to-sales (P/S) ratio.

Both of these stocks tap into consumer shopping habits, and Five Below has a unique value proposition with its pricing. But the key difference between the two is that Etsy operates on an asset-light model, while Five Below operates on an asset-heavy model. Between the two, I would lean more toward owning Etsy, given the uncertainty the markets are facing heading into 2027.

Five Below's model relies on opening more stores, acquiring and selling more inventory, hiring more employees, and spending more on logistics. If the economy faces a downturn, that hurts that model hard. In comparison, Etsy relies on sellers using its digital platform, which largely just requires connecting more sellers with buyers and doesn't require opening hundreds of stores a year for revenue growth.

As of now, Etsy is seeing revenue growth, as evidenced by its 2026 third-quarter results. Sales increased 11% from the prior-year period to $692 million, and net income totaled $114 million.

In terms of risks, one of the largest is the same for Etsy as it is for Five Below: a slowdown in consumer spending. Still, Etsy is better equipped to handle that with its asset-light model.

What causes a sudden drop in phone battery percentage?

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An aging battery or a recent software update could be the reasons behind your phone's sudden battery drops.

Few things are more frustrating than noticing that your phone's battery is draining faster than usual. It gets to a point where it can no longer last a full day away from the charger. Under normal conditions, battery drain depends on what you're doing on your phone. Lighter uses like browsing the web and listening to music use less power, while taking lots of photos and playing games tend to drain your battery faster. But when your phone's battery suddenly drops without heavy use, something might be wrong.

At the same time, that doesn't mean your phone's battery is dying. Software can also cause your battery to drain faster, like when you install an iOS or Android update, or when an app is constantly running in the background. If you've been noticing that your phone's battery is draining faster, here's what you can try to fix it.

Your phone doesn't know exactly how much charge is left in the battery. In fact, it estimates that number based on the battery's voltage and current, and sometimes that estimate is far off from reality. Eventually, the software recalculates it, and that's when you see the battery percentage suddenly drop by a significant amount instead of gradually. This happens more often with batteries in poor condition. As lithium-ion batteries age chemically, they no longer retain their full charge capacity and may also fail when the phone needs a power boost to perform a demanding task, like opening the camera or using GPS. As a result, your phone may think the battery is much lower than it actually is.

Smartphones have mechanisms to prevent this from happening. On iPhones, Apple has implemented a system that detects when the battery fails to reduce performance in order to avoid these spikes in power consumption. Temperature also has a major impact, as most phones are rated to operate at temperatures between 0° and 35° C (32° to 95° F). Using your phone at temperatures above that range can significantly reduce battery life. When your phone gets too hot, the battery will most likely drain much faster. It's best to stop using it until it returns to a normal temperature. Don't put it in a refrigerator, though, as the introduction of cold air can create condensation within the warmer phone.

Also, it's perfectly normal to see your phone's battery drain a little faster for a few hours or even days after an update. Both iOS and Android run a series of optimizations to put all the files in their proper places, and this increases battery consumption. The good news is that this is temporary, and everything goes back to normal once the indexing process is complete. A buggy app or a process constantly running in the background, especially if it uses your location, will also drain your phone's battery quickly.

Start by closing apps running in the background to see if your battery usage returns to normal. It's also worth taking a look at your privacy settings and limiting access to your location to only when you have an app open. This prevents your phone from constantly triggering the GPS. That alone should have a significant impact on reducing battery consumption. Also, check your phone's battery settings to see exactly which apps are draining your battery the most — both iOS and Android provide details on this.

Pixel 6 and later models have a Battery Diagnostics tool under Settings. On a Samsung Galaxy, open Settings, then Battery and tap View Details. For iPhone users, it's as simple as going to Settings and opening the Battery menu. These menus usually include details about your phone's battery health. Apple, for example, says that the latest iPhones are designed to retain 80 percent of their original battery capacity through up to 1,000 charge cycles. If your phone's battery health is below 80 percent, you should probably replace it with a new one — which, at least, is much cheaper than buying a new phone.

Kazakhstan Targets Tokenized Government Bonds After $22 Million Note Sale

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Kazakhstan wants to move from experiments with tokenised central-bank notes to government bonds recorded using blockchain infrastructure. Officials are targeting the first quarter of 2027 for a sovereign-debt issuance, following a transaction this month that placed about $22 million of central-bank notes in tokenised form. It is a concrete development for the real-world-asset industry, although an important boundary remains: the government-bond deal is still a plan, not a completed sale.

That distinction is essential because tokenised securities announcements often mix prototypes, pilots and fully issued instruments. Kazakhstan has evidence of one completed step and an expressed ambition for the next.

As CoinDesk reported, the National Bank of Kazakhstan sold a total of 50 billion tenge in 91-day notes, with 10 billion tenge — approximately $22.3 million — issued through a tokenised arrangement. The notes carried a weighted-average yield of about 15.72%, and aggregate demand was roughly 1.4 times the offered amount.

Those details matter because they place the blockchain component within a recognisable debt-market transaction. Investors still care about the issuer, maturity, yield, settlement rules and custody. A digital token does not erase credit risk or replace the financial obligations underlying a security. It is a different technical representation and transfer mechanism for an instrument that remains subject to conventional legal and market questions.

National Bank Governor Timur Suleimenov outlined an ambition to work with the country's Finance Ministry on tokenised sovereign bonds, with the first quarter of 2027 described as the likely timing. Central-bank notes and Finance Ministry bonds are not interchangeable: they have different issuers and may serve different investor or monetary-policy purposes. The future deal should therefore be assessed on its own terms.

The Kazakhstan Stock Exchange has said its digital-asset platform used Solana, with BitGo providing custody infrastructure, for the note initiative. It would be premature to conclude that those same providers are definitively selected for the government-bond transaction. Technical participation, permissions and investor eligibility need to be confirmed when official issuance documentation is available.

Supporters of tokenisation point to shorter settlement cycles, clearer ownership records and the possibility of integrating securities with programmable workflows. Those benefits are plausible, but whether they materialise depends on legal recognition, interoperability with payment systems and the behaviour of market participants. A blockchain that records a bond is only one component of a working securities market.

For Kazakhstan, the experiment also sits alongside wider work on digital assets. NewsBTC previously reported on Kraken's in-principle regulatory approval in Kazakhstan, a separate initiative that highlights the country's interest in building financial infrastructure around the sector. Neither development means every planned crypto service has already received full authorisation.

The next useful documents will set out who can buy the bonds, how transfers are restricted, which custodians are responsible for assets and how payments are settled. They should also explain whether investors can hold instruments outside a single controlled platform and how disputes would be resolved. Those are the details that distinguish a market-ready security from a demonstration.

The same questions apply to other tokenised securities projects integrating shareholder rights. Kazakhstan has moved beyond slides and into a completed note placement. The proposed government-bond issuance will be more convincing if it brings the same clarity about the legal and operational substance behind the token.
